=== page 3 === [running header: Michigan K-12 Social Studies Standards | MICHIGAN DEPARTMENT OF EDUCATION]
u34 THE GOALS OF SOCIAL STUDIES
u35 Introduction
u36 Michigan’s Social Studies Content Expectations describe what students should know and be able to do in order to succeed in college, career, and civic life. In 2013, the State of Michigan began revising the content expectations and involved educators from local, ISD, university, and state-level organizations. The project was focused on updating the existing 2007 standards around the charge of “clearer, fewer, and higher” and the result of this work is presented here.
u37 Writing teams met on a regular basis throughout the revision process and several opportunities for public review and commentary were provided. Sessions took place around the state in 2015, 2018, and again in 2019. As a result, a diverse representation of Michigan’s educators and citizens provided additional feedback, which was used to shape the final version of this document.
u38 This document is not intended to be a state curriculum. The revised content in the standards is coupled with the Arc of Inquiry and skills delineated in the C3 Framework. In a “local control” state such as Michigan, each district can use the document as it sees fit to revise curriculum and create a foundation from which it can continue to improve instruction.
u39 Purpose of Social Studies
u40 The purpose of social studies is to promote the knowledge, skills, intellectual processes, and dispositions required of people to be actively engaged in fulfilling their responsibility of civic participation. As members of a culturally diverse, democratic society in an interdependent world, young people need to learn how to make informed and reasoned decisions for the public good. Social studies fosters a renewed and reinvigorated commitment to the ideal, “government of the people, by the people, and for the people,” as expressed by President Lincoln in his Gettysburg Address. The expectations outlined below are designed to fulfill that purpose.
u41 Literacy in Social Studies
u42 The digital revolution has fostered a sizable shift not only in how students acquire information, but how educators make social studies more relevant and meaningful. Teachers are welcoming into their classrooms students who have grown up in a world where multiple modes of communication and interaction are an indispensable part of everyday life. Instant communication has made distances between locations practically invisible; the pace of change is now at a staggering rate, and there is a sizable and expanding role of civic participation. As a result, students need to be equipped with a more sophisticated level of literacy than ever before — one that transcends basic technical and functional knowledge and skills.
u43 For many, literacy means different things from a wide variety of perspectives. One constant, however, is that the notion of literacy is often associated with the mastery of the technical skills of oral and written communication, dialogue, and questioning. Today’s society demands an urgent need to move beyond content-based teaching and the application of discipline-specific skill sets (e.g., thinking like a historian, geographer, economist). Critical literacy is the next cerebral step as students move toward an approach to see and “read” themselves and the world.
u44 Embedded in literacy practices, critical literacy provides opportunities for students to utilize an integrated approach. Critical literacy has been defined as “learning to read and write as part of the process of becoming conscious of one’s experiences as historically constructed within specific power relations” (Anderson & Irvine, 1982). In simpler terms, critical literacy is about how students evaluate society and possess the necessary abilities and the desire to interact with the world. The combined approach of the skill sets of disciplinary literacy along with the tools of critical literacy for critical thinking empowers students with multiple perspectives and questioning habits. It encourages them to think and take informed action on their decisions through dialogue, civic participation, and their daily decisions about how to live so that they can help make their world better.

u48 The Responsibilities of Civic Participation
u49 (x=45) Responsible citizenship requires active participation in our communities. Therefore, social studies instruction should engage students so they simultaneously learn about civic participation while being involved in the civic life of their communities, our state, and our nation. Social studies prepares students to participate in political life, to serve their communities, and to conduct themselves responsibly.

u66 • Understanding American government and politics to make informed decisions about governing and their community. (Civic Perspective)
u67 • Using knowledge of the production, distribution, and consumption of goods and services to make personal, career, and societal decisions about the use of resources. (Economic Perspective)
u68 • Using methods of social science investigation to answer questions about society. (Inquiry)
u69 • Knowing how, when, and where to construct and express reasoned positions on public issues. (Public Discourse and Decision Making)
u70 • Acting constructively to further the public good (Civic Participation)

u74 (x=30) Dimension 1: Developing Questions and
u75 (x=306) The College, Career, and Civic Life (C3) Framework was developed by more than twenty-six state agencies and social studies organizations over the course of several years. It introduces an Arc of Inquiry that a teacher may find valuable when planning social studies instruction. Inquiry, as an instructional practice, can be a powerful tool for local- or site-level curriculum planning and development, or for teachers in refining their practice.

=== page 5 === [running header: Michigan K-12 Social Studies Standards | MICHIGAN DEPARTMENT OF EDUCATION]
u91 (x=36) It is in the K-12 social studies classrooms that the youth comprising our future will learn about civil discourse, the history of our families, schools, communities, state, nation and world, and how to be a productive member of society.
u92 Inquiry is at the heart of social studies. It is through identification of questions and problems, studying various disciplinary lenses, learning to use and evaluate sources and evidence, and communicating possible conclusions that students can be prepared to face the challenges of the modern world.
u93 Social studies is composed of deep and enduring understandings, concepts, and skills from the disciplines. From studying questions like “Who makes up a community?” to grappling with bigger issues like “Can one person change the world?”, the acquisition of both content knowledge and skills is essential.
u94 Social studies emphasizes skills and practices as preparation for democratic decision making. Strong content knowledge, like the standards outlined in the Michigan Social Studies Standards, is only one part of preparing students for life beyond the walls of a school. That content knowledge must be coupled with strong, foundational skills that prepare students to navigate a complex and ever-changing world.
u95 Social studies education should have direct and explicit connections to other standards, both local and national. The Michigan Social Studies Standards outline content that can be further developed at the local level with the addition of local examples. By including portions of the C3 Framework alongside Michigan’s revised standards, districts now have a blueprint for the integration of literacy, social studies content, and other disciplines such as science, art, and the humanities.
u96 The Critical Component: Instructional Shifts of the Frameworks
u97 The C3 Framework represents a substantial shift in the way that social studies was most commonly taught in the past. To meet the changing needs of students in the Information Age, and to prepare them for the challenges of a dynamic world environment, the following instructional shifts are necessary:
u98 1. Inquiry should be a primary form of instruction in all social studies classes.
u99 2. Students (and teachers) should craft investigative questions that matter.
u100 3. Teachers should establish a collaborative context to support student inquiry.
u101 4. Teachers should integrate content and skills meaningfully and in a rigorous manner.
u102 5. Teachers should help students articulate disciplinary literacy practices and outcomes (thinking, reading, writing, speaking like a historian, like a geographer, like an economist, etc.).
u103 6. Teachers should provide, and help students develop, tangible opportunities to take informed action.
u104 Inquiry can be a powerful tool for teaching the content outlined in Michigan’s Grade Level Content Expectations. As humans, we are naturally prone to questioning as we try to make sense of the world around us. While the C3 Framework is not assessed on state-level assessments, such as the M-STEP, it provides guidance for teachers and students on how to practice structured inquiry at the classroom level. It is set up around an instructional arc outlined below, with more information available by downloading the full document from the National Council for the Social Studies. A full copy of the C3 Framework can be found online.
u105 Inquiry Arc
u106 The inquiry arc highlights the structure of and rationale for the organization of the C3 Framework’s four dimensions. The arc focuses on the nature of inquiry in general and the pursuit of knowledge through questions in particular. The C3 Framework, alongside the Michigan Social Studies Content Expectations, connect with the Michigan ELA Standards.
u107 Dimensions and Subsections
u108 The C3 Framework is organized into the four dimensions, which support a robust social studies program rooted in inquiry.
u109 Dimensions 2, 3, and 4 are further broken down into subsections. For example, Dimension 2, Applying

u121 Unique Structure of Dimension 2
u122 Dimension 2 has an additional layer of three to four categories within each disciplinary subsection. These categories provide an organizing mechanism for the foundational content and skills within each discipline. For example, within the subsection of economics, there are four categories: (1) Economic Decision Making; (2) Exchange and Markets; (3) The National Economy; and (4) The Global Economy.

u134 (x=112) MICHIGAN’S GRADE LEVEL CONTENT EXPECTATIONS
u135 (x=226) FOR SOCIAL STUDIES
u136 MICHIGAN’S SOCIAL STUDIES STANDARDS
u137 The purpose of social studies instruction is to develop social understanding and civic efficacy. The Grade Level Content Expectations (GLCE) balance disciplinary content with processes and skills that contribute to responsible citizenship and form a foundation for high school social studies coursework.
u138 The disciplinary knowledge found in this document can be used by students to construct meaning through understanding of powerful ideas drawn from the disciplines of history, geography, civics and government, and economics.
u139 Effective social studies instruction and assessment incorporate methods of inquiry, involve public discourse and decision making, and provide opportunities for citizen involvement. These methods in the updated standards fit well with the four dimensions of the C3 Framework.
u140 The K-12 Social Studies GLCE was revised to meet these goals:
u141 Increasing rigor and ensuring they were challenging enough to equip students with necessary skills to succeed at the next grade level, while still representing the essential core content of a discipline.
u142 Providing more clarity to teachers and educational stakeholders. Standards need to be widely understood and accepted by teachers, parents, school boards, and others who have a stake in the quality of schooling.
u143 Specific enough to provide sufficient detail for districts who are developing curricula and teachers planning instruction, while providing enough focus to delineate which facts, concepts, and skills should be emphasized at each grade level.
u144 Moving from simple to complex, from concrete to abstract, the Michigan standards needed to clearly delineate a progression of both knowledge and skills across grade levels, with each grade level providing a brick on the road toward mastery of the high school content.
u145 Reflecting a coherent structure of the discipline and/or revealing significant relationships among the strands, as appropriate.
u146 Accurate enough for all Michigan students to see themselves.
u147 UNDERSTANDING SOCIAL STUDIES GLCE CODING
u148 (x=36) In use since the 2007 standards, each social studies GLCE code is made up of four parts: the grade, the standard category, the standard, and the expectation. In grades K-4, the “standard category” is described by discipline; in grades 5 through high school, “standard category” is described by topic. As a result, K-4 expectations are organized using the standard categories, and do not use the standard codes listed in the K-12 organizational chart.
u149 (x=153) 6 – E 2 . 3 . 1
u150 Grade Standard Category Standard Expectation
u151 K-4 expectations are organized by discipline and standard category, standard, and expectation.
u152 Kindergarten example: K – G1.0.2 = Kindergarten, 1st Geography Standard Category, 2nd Expectation
u153 4th Grade example: 4 – C5.0.3 = Grade 4, 5th Civics Standard Category, 3rd Expectation
u154 (The “0” is used as a place holder and indicates that K-4 expectations are organized using the standard categories, and do not use the standard codes listed in the K-12 organizational chart).
u155 5th and 8th grades focus on an integrated study of United States history. The expectations are organized by U.S. History and Geography (USHG) era. The code indicates the era, the standard, and the expectation.
u156 5th Grade example: 5 – U3.2.1 = Grade 5, 3rd USHG Era, 2nd Standard, 1st Expectation
u157 6th and 7th grades focus on an integrated study of the world. The expectations are organized by discipline and standard category (or World History and Geography [WHG] era), standard, and expectation.

u162 MICHIGAN’S PROCESS AND SKILLS STANDARDS
u163 Michigan’s Process and Skills Standards identify the inquiry, communication, evaluation, and decision- making abilities that can be developed in all disciplines and at many grade levels. Local districts and teachers integrate work on inquiry processes and communication skills throughout the curriculum in ways that best respond to the needs of the district’s children.

u192 MICHIGAN CONTENT EXPECTATIONS
u193 Michigan Process and Skills Standards have been changed from the 2007 standards in several ways. First, they are fewer and clearer to provide teachers with more focused guidelines. Second, Process and Skill Standards have now been included for elementary, middle school, and high school in a developmentally appropriate manner instead of just for high school. Last, they specifically include the development of compelling and supporting questions.

u214 Several considerations are important as teachers use the GLCE to plan instruction.
u215 Integrate acquisition of content (in the GLCE) with process and skill development. Development of basic skills in interpreting text, data, graphs, and maps in elementary and middle schools is important for success in high school. Development of basic citizenship and discussion skills, while never tested on state exams, is nonetheless critical for success in and out of high school.
u216 Active social studies inquiry is essential. The Arc of Inquiry from College, Career, and Civic Life (C3) is a description of a process that helps students develop the kind of reasoned and informed decision– making skills needed for active participation in American society. Using the Arc of Inquiry begins with the development of compelling questions. Exemplars for the use of compelling questions will be included in the instructional material being developed to accompany the revised standards.
u217 The GLCE is a content guide, not a curriculum organizer; it does not specify lessons, units, or a curriculum sequence. World Geography can be taught regionally or thematically. History can be taught past to present, or present to past. One teacher may develop a community activity at the beginning of the year to help develop a sense of purpose, and another might wait until year’s end as part of a capstone project.
u218 On numerous occasions, the expectations will include examples to help clarify teachable content. These specific examples are suggestions. Educators may use other examples to meet the expectations or to guide instruction and the creation of a local curriculum and resources. Specific examples included for each standard are clearly labeled underneath each standard by using the language “examples may include but are not limited to.” These examples are not assessable outside of a stimulus text on state summative assessments. The focus of a state assessment question will be the language and content delineated in the content expectation itself. In the example below, the content standard is about the origins of the American education system. Benjamin Franklin, Benjamin Rush, Noah Webster, and Horace Mann are just four of the many examples that could be used when teaching the standard.
u219 (x=54) 8 – U4.3.1
u220 (x=153) Explain the origins of the American education system.
u221 Examples may include but are not limited to: Benjamin Franklin, Benjamin Rush, Noah Webster, and Horace Mann.
=== page 10 === [running header: Michigan K-12 Social Studies Standards | MICHIGAN DEPARTMENT OF EDUCATION]
u224 10 THE SOCIAL STUDIES STANDARDS AND MICHIGAN LAW:
u225 Michigan Public Act No. 170 of 2016 states: “Beginning in the 2016-2017 school year, the board of a school district or board of directors of a public school academy shall ensure that the school district’s or public school academy’s social studies curriculum for grades 8 to 12 includes age- and grade-appropriate instruction about genocide, including, but not limited to, the Holocaust and the Armenian Genocide. The legislature recommends a combined total of 6 hours of this instruction during grades 8 to 12.”
u226 Careful attention, review, and revision work was conducted to ensure that the mandate of Public Act No. 170 of 2016 was met with the revisions to the Michigan K-12 Standards for Social Studies. The law also states that genocide instruction may take place over time, between grade levels, and across classes and disciplines. A student may read a compelling novel such as Night by Elie Wiesel and learn about the Holocaust in both the context of their English/Language Arts class and either their high school World History and Geography Course (HS-WHG 7.2.3, 7.2.6) or their high school United States History and Geography course (HS-US 7.2.4). A student may also study the Armenian Genocide in both courses, with complementary social studies instruction found in HS-WHG 7.2.1 and 7.2.6.
u227 Opportunities to meet the requirement of this law exist both within the confines of the revised Michigan K-12 Standards for Social Studies and beyond the boundaries of the social studies classroom.

u231 K-2 OVERVIEW
u232 [T1 R1 C1] K-2 Grade-Specific Contexts
u233 [T1 R2 C1] Kindergarten
u234 [T1 R2 C2] Myself and Others
u235 [T1 R2 C3] Using a familiar context for five- and six-year-olds, kindergarteners learn about the social studies disciplines (history, geography, civics and government, and economics) through the lens of “Myself and Others.”
u236 [T1 R3 C1] 1st
u237 [T1 R3 C2] Families and Schools
u238 [T1 R3 C3] Students continue to explore the social studies disciplines of history, geography, civics and government, and economics through an integrated approach using the context of school and families. This is the students’ first introduction to social institutions.
u239 [T1 R4 C1] 2nd
u240 [T1 R4 C2] The Local Community
u241 [T1 R4 C3] Students continue the integrative approach to social studies through the context of the local community. Students are introduced to a social environment larger than their immediate surroundings.

=== page 12 ===
u254 THE ARC OF INQUIRY: GRADES K-2
u255 Dimension 1: Developing Questions and Planning Inquiries Central to a rich social studies experience is the capability for developing questions that can frame and advance an inquiry. Those questions come in two forms: compelling and supporting questions.
u256 Individually and collaboratively, students construct compelling questions and:
u257 • explain why the compelling question is important to students.
u258 • identify disciplinary ideas associated with a compelling question.
u259 • identify facts and concepts associated with a compelling question.
u260 • make connections between supporting questions and compelling questions.
u261 • determine the kinds of sources that will be helpful in answering compelling and supporting questions.
u262 Dimension 2: Applying Disciplinary Concepts and Tools The four disciplines within social studies provide the intellectual context for studying how humans have interacted with each other and with the environment over time. Each of these disciplines — civics, economics, geography, and history — offers a unique way of thinking and organizing knowledge as well as systems for verifying knowledge. Dimension 2 focuses on the disciplinary concepts and tools students need to understand and apply as they study the specific content described in Michigan’s state standards.
u263 Dimension 3: Evaluating Sources and Using Evidence Dimension 3 includes the skills students need to analyze information and come to conclusions in an inquiry. These skills focus on gathering and evaluating sources, and then developing claims and using evidence to support these claims.
u264 Individually and collaboratively, students:
u265 • gather relevant information from one or two sources while using the origin and structure to guide their selection.
u266 • evaluate a source by distinguishing between fact and opinion.
u267 Dimension 4: Communicating Conclusions and Taking Informed Action Students should construct and communicate claims for a variety of purposes and audiences. These audiences may range from the school classroom to the larger public community.
u268 Individually and collaboratively, students:
u269 • construct an argument with reasons.
u270 • construct explanations using correct sequence and relevant information.
u271 • present a summary of an argument using print, oral, and digital technologies.
u272 • ask and answer questions about arguments.
u273 • ask and answer questions about explanations.
u274 • identify and explain a range of local, regional, and global problems and some ways in which people are trying to address these problems.
u275 • identify ways to take action and help address local, regional, and global problems.
u276 • use listening, consensus building, and voting procedures to decide on and take action in their classrooms.
u277 [T1 R1 C2] Sample K-2 Compelling and Supporting Questions
u278 [T1 R2 C2] Kindergarten
u279 [T1 R2 C3] How do we get along with others?
u280 [T1 R2 C4] 1) Why do I have rules at home and at school? 2) Why can’t I have everything I want? 3) What are some fair ways to make decisions in a group? Standards connections: K – C1.0.1, K – C2.0.2, K – C5.0.1
u281 [T1 R3 C2] 1st
u282 [T1 R3 C3] Why is it important to learn about the past?
u283 [T1 R3 C4] 1) What historical sources can you use to learn about family and school life in the past? 2) What conclusions can you draw about family life in the past? 3) What conclusions can you draw about school life in the past? Standards connections: 1 – H2.0.1, 1 – H2.0.2, 1 – H2.0.3, 1 – H2.0.4
u284 [T1 R4 C2] 2nd
u285 [T1 R4 C3] How do people work together in a community?
u286 [T1 R4 C4] 1) How does scarcity affect people? 2) How can people make good economic choices? 3) How do people use resources to produce goods and services? 4) Why do people trade? Standards connections: 2 – E1.0.2, 2 – E1.0.3, 2 – E1.0.4, 2 – E1.0.5

u289 SOCIAL STUDIES PROCESS AND SKILLS STANDARDS K-2
u290 P1 READING AND COMMUNICATION – READ AND COMMUNICATE EFFECTIVELY
u291 P1.1 Use appropriate strategies to read and interpret basic social science tables, graphs, graphics, maps, and texts.
u292 P1.2 Differentiate between primary and secondary source documents.
u293 P1.3 Express social science ideas or information in written, spoken, and graphic forms including tables, line graphs, bar graphs, and maps.
u294 P1.4 Identify point of view and bias.
u295 P2 INQUIRY, RESEARCH, AND ANALYSIS
u296 P2.1 Use compelling and supporting questions to investigate social studies problems.
u297 P2.2 Differentiate between compelling questions and supporting questions.
u298 P2.3 Use supporting questions to help answer compelling social studies questions.
u299 P2.4 Know how to find relevant evidence from a variety of sources.
u300 P2.5 Use data presented in social science tables, graphs, graphics, maps, and texts to answer compelling and supporting questions.
u301 P3 PUBLIC DISCOURSE AND DECISION MAKING
u302 P3.1 State an issue as a question of public policy and discuss possible solutions from different perspectives.
u303 P3.2 Apply Democratic Values or Constitutional Principles to support a position on an issue.
u304 P3.3 Construct an argument and justify a decision supported with evidence.
u305 P3.4 Explain the challenges people have faced and actions they have taken to address issues at different times and places.
u306 P4 CIVIC PARTICIPATION
u307 P4.1 Act out of the rule of law and hold others to the same standard.
u308 P4.2 Assess options for individuals and groups to plan and conduct activities intended to advance views on matters of public policy.
u309 P4.3 Explain different strategies students and others could take to address problems and predict possible results.
u310 P4.4 Use democratic procedures to make decisions on civic issues in the school or classroom.

u312 SOCIAL STUDIES CONTENT EXPECTATIONS: KINDERGARTEN
u313 HISTORY
u314 Individually and collaboratively, students will engage in planned inquiries to investigate ways people learn about the past.
u315 H2 Living and Working Together
u316 Use historical thinking to understand the past.
u317 (x=69) K – H2.0.1
u318 (x=155) Distinguish among the past, present, and future.
u319 (x=69) K – H2.0.2
u320 (x=155) Create a timeline using events from their own lives.
u321 (x=69) K – H2.0.3
u322 (x=155) Describe ways people learn about the past.
u323 GEOGRAPHY
u324 Individually and collaboratively, students will engage in planned inquiries to investigate how the environment provides for people’s needs and wants.
u325 G1 The World in Spatial Terms
u326 Use geographic representations to acquire, process, and report information from a spatial perspective.
u327 (x=69) K – G1.0.1
u328 (x=155) Recognize that maps and globes represent places.
u329 (x=69) K – G1.0.2
u330 (x=155) Use directions or positional words to identify significant locations in
u331 (x=69) the classroom.
u332 Examples may include but are not limited to: up/down, in/out, above/below, left/right.
u333 G2 Places and Regions
u334 Understand how regions are created from common physical and human characteristics.
u335 (x=69) K – G2.0.1
u336 (x=155) Identify and describe places in the immediate environment.
u337 Examples may include but are not limited to: classroom, home, playground.
u338 G5 Environment and Society
u339 Understand the effects of human-environment interactions.
u340 (x=69) K – G5.0.1
u341 (x=155) Describe ways in which the environment provides for basic human
u342 (x=69) needs and wants.
u343 Examples may include but are not limited to: food, shelter, clothing.

u642 C5 Civic Participation
u643 Explain important rights and how, when, and where members of American society demonstrate their responsibilities by actively participating in civic life.
u644 (x=53) 2 – C5.0.1
u645 (x=144) Identify ways in which people participate in community decisions.

u647 (x=144) Distinguish between personal and civic responsibilities and explain why
u648 (x=53) they are important in community life.
u649 Examples may include but are not limited to: taking care of your dog, recycling, caring for family members (personal responsibility), getting a dog license, putting re cycling in the appropriate place, serving on a jury (civic responsibility).
u650 (x=53) 2 – C5.0.3
u651 (x=144) Design and participate in community improvement projects that help
u652 (x=53) or inform others.

u701 3RD-5TH GRADE OVERVIEW
u702 [T1 R1 C1] 3rd-5th Grade-Specific Contexts
u703 [T1 R2 C1] 3rd
u704 [T1 R2 C2] Michigan Studies
u705 [T1 R2 C3] Students explore the social studies disciplines of history, geography, civics and government, and economics through the context of Michigan studies.
u706 [T1 R3 C1] 4th
u707 [T1 R3 C2] U.S. Studies
u708 [T1 R3 C3] Using the context of the state of Michigan post statehood and the United States, 4th grade students learn significant social studies concepts within an increasingly complex social environment. They examine fundamental concepts in geography, civics and government, and economics organized by topic, region, or issue.
u709 [T1 R4 C1] 5th
u710 [T1 R4 C2] Integrated U.S. History
u711 [T1 R4 C3] Building upon the geography, civics and government, and economics concepts of the United States mastered in 4th grade and historical inquiry from earlier grades, the 5th grade expectations begin a more discipline-centered approach concentrating on the early history of the United States. Students begin their study of American history with Indigenous Peoples before the arrival of European explorers and conclude with the adoption of the Bill of Rights in 1791. Although the content expectations are organized by historical era, they build upon students’ understanding of the other social studies disciplines from earlier grades and require students to apply these concepts within the context of American history.

u719 [T1 R7 C2] The World in Spatial Terms Use geographic representations to acquire, process, and report information from a spatial perspective. Places and Regions Understand how regions are created from common physical and human characteristics. Human Systems Understand how human activities help shape the Earth’s surface. Environment and Society Understand the effects of human-environment interactions.
u720 [T1 R7 C4] Purposes of Government Explain why people create governments. Democratic Values and Constitutional Principles of American Government Understand Democratic Values and Constitutional Principles of American government. Structure and Function of Government Describe the structure of government in the United States and how it functions. Civic Participation Explain important rights and how, when, and where people can demonstrate their responsibilities by participating in government.
u721 [T1 R7 C5] Market Economy Use fundamental principles and concepts of economics to understand economic activity in a market economy. National Economy Use fundamental principles and concepts of economics to understand economic activity in the United States. International Economy Use fundamental principles and concepts of economics to understand economic activity in the global economy.
u722 [T1 R7 C6] Identifying and Analyzing Public Issues Clearly state a problem as a public policy issue, analyze various perspectives, and generate and evaluate possible alternative resolutions. Persuasive Communication Communicate a reasoned position on a public issue. Civic Participation Act constructively to further the public good.

u737 THE ARC OF INQUIRY: GRADES 3-5
u738 Dimension 1: Developing Questions and Planning Inquiries Central to a rich social studies experience is the capability for developing questions that can frame and advance an inquiry. Those questions come in two forms: compelling and supporting questions.
u739 Individually and collaboratively, students construct compelling questions and:
u740 • explain why compelling questions are important to others (e.g., peers, adults).
u741 • identify disciplinary concepts and ideas associated with a compelling question that are open to different interpretations.
u742 • identify the disciplinary concepts and ideas associated with a supporting question that are open to interpretation.
u743 • explain how supporting questions help answer compelling questions in an inquiry.
u744 • determine the kinds of sources that will be helpful in answering compelling and supporting questions, taking into consideration the different opinions people have about how to answer the questions.
u745 Dimension 2: Applying Disciplinary Concepts and Tools The four disciplines within social studies provide the intellectual context for studying how humans have interacted with each other and with the environment over time. Each of these disciplines — civics, economics, geography, and history — offers a unique way of thinking and organizing knowledge as well as systems for verifying knowledge. Dimension 2 focuses on the disciplinary concepts and tools students need to understand and apply as they study the specific content described in Michigan’s state standards.
u746 Dimension 3: Evaluating Sources and Using Evidence Dimension 3 includes the skills students need to analyze information and come to conclusions in an inquiry. These skills focus on gathering and evaluating sources, and then developing claims and using evidence to support these claims.
u747 Individually and collaboratively, students:
u748 • gather relevant information from multiple sources while using the origin, structure, and context to guide the selection.
u749 • use distinctions among fact and opinion to determine the credibility of multiple sources.
u750 • identify evidence that draws information from multiple sources in response to compelling questions.
u751 • use evidence to develop claims in response to compelling questions.
u752 Dimension 4: Communicating Conclusions and Taking Informed Action Students should construct and communicate claims for a variety of purposes and audiences. These audiences may range from the school classroom to the larger public community.
u753 Individually and collaboratively, students:
u754 • construct arguments using claims and evidence from multiple sources.
u755 • construct explanations using reasoning, correct sequence, examples, and details with relevant information and data.
u756 • present a summary of arguments and explanations to others outside the classroom using print and oral technologies (e.g., posters, essays, letters, debates, speeches, and reports) and digital technologies (e.g., Internet, social media, and digital documentary).
u757 • critique arguments.
u758 • critique explanations.
u759 • draw on disciplinary concepts to explain the challenges people have faced and opportunities they have created, in addressing local, regional, and global problems at various times and places.
u760 • explain different strategies and approaches students and others could take in working alone and together to address local, regional, and global problems, and predict possible results of their actions; use listening, consensus-building, and voting procedures to decide on and take action in their classrooms.
u761 • use a range of deliberative and democratic procedures to make decisions about and act on civic problems in their classrooms and schools.
=== page 28 === [running header: Michigan K-12 Social Studies Standards | MICHIGAN DEPARTMENT OF EDUCATION]
u763 [T1 R1 C1] Sample 3rd-5th Grade Compelling and Supporting Questions
u764 [T1 R2 C1] 3rd
u765 [T1 R2 C2] What makes Michigan special?
u766 [T1 R2 C3] 1) How is the geography of Michigan similar to or different from the geography of other states? 2) How is the geography different in different places in Michigan? 3) How does Michigan’s location in North America influence its resources? Standards Connection: 3 – G2.0.1, 3 – G2.0.2, 3 – G4.0.1, 3 – G4.0.2, 3 – G4.0.3, 3 – G4.0.4
u767 [T1 R3 C1] 4th
u768 [T1 R3 C2] How does the U.S. economy work?
u769 [T1 R3 C3] 1) What are the characteristics of a market economy? 2) How does a market economy work? 3) How does specialization and division of labor increase productivity? 4) How is the U.S. economy impacted by global competition? Standards Connection: 3 – E1.0.1, 3 – E1.0.2, 3 – E1.0.3, 3 – E1.0.4, 3 – E1.0.5, 3 – E1.0.6, 3 – E1.0.7, 3 – E1.0.8, 3 – E2.0.1
u770 [T1 R4 C1] 5th
u771 [T1 R4 C2] Does geography determine destiny?
u772 [T1 R4 C3] 1) What conditions and connections determine the fate of a settlement? 2) How did Europeans benefit from the Triangular Trade and what impact did it have on the lives of West Africans? 3) How and why did different colonial regions develop differently? Standards Connection: 5 – U2.1.1, 5 – U2.1.2, 5 – U2.1.3, 5 – U2.1.4

u776 SOCIAL STUDIES PROCESS AND SKILLS STANDARDS 3-5
u777 P1 READING AND COMMUNICATION – READ AND COMMUNICATE EFFECTIVELY
u778 P1.1 Use appropriate strategies to read and interpret basic social science tables, graphs, graphics, maps, and texts.
u779 P1.2 Differentiate between primary and secondary source documents.
u780 P1.3 Express social science ideas or information in written, spoken, and graphic forms including tables, line graphs, bar graphs, and maps.
u781 P1.4 Identify point of view and bias.
u782 P2 INQUIRY, RESEARCH, AND ANALYSIS
u783 P2.1 Use compelling and supporting questions to investigate social studies problems.
u784 P2.2 Differentiate between compelling questions and supporting questions.
u785 P2.3 Use supporting questions to help answer compelling social studies questions.
u786 P2.4 Know how to find relevant evidence from a variety of sources.
u787 P2.5 Use data presented in social science tables, graphs, graphics, maps, and texts to answer compelling and supporting questions.
u788 P3 PUBLIC DISCOURSE AND DECISION MAKING
u789 P3.1 State an issue as a question of public policy and discuss possible solutions from different perspectives.
u790 P3.2 Apply Democratic Values or Constitutional Principles to support a position on an issue.
u791 P3.3 Construct an argument and justify a decision supported with evidence.
u792 P3.4 Explain the challenges people have faced and actions they have taken to address issues at different times and places.
u793 P4 CIVIC PARTICIPATION
u794 P4.1 Act out of the rule of law and hold others to the same standard.
u795 P4.2 Assess options for individuals and groups to plan and conduct activities intended to advance views on matters of public policy.
u796 P4.3 Explain different strategies students and others could take to address problems and predict possible results.
u797 P4.4 Use democratic procedures to make decisions on civic issues in the school or classroom.

u865 (x=53) agriculture, forestry, manufacturing, services and tourism, and research and develop ment, and explain the factors influencing the location of these economic activities.

u867 Examples of factors influencing location may include but are not limited to: primary industries located near natural resources; manufacturing influenced by acces sibility to resources, labor, markets, and capital; and services, which are often located close to markets.

u1015 (x=58) 4 – H3.0.7

u1220 Note: U.S. historians, history books, history standards, and the peoples themselves have used, at one time or another, “Na- tive American” and “American Indian,” while Canadian history uses “First Peoples” to refer to inhabitants of North America prior to European exploration, conquest, and settlement. While we are using “Indigenous Peoples” throughout the content expectations, students should be familiar with the different names and specific tribal identities as they will likely encounter variations over the course of their studies.

u1223 SOCIAL STUDIES CONTENT EXPECTATIONS: GRADE FIVE
u1224 U1 USHG ERA 1 – BEGINNINGS TO 1620
u1225 Individually and collaboratively, students will engage in planned inquiries to understand how early European exploration and colonization resulted in cultural and ecological interactions among previously unconnected peoples.
u1226 U1.1 Indigenous Peoples’ Lives in the Americas
u1227 Describe the lives of the Indigenous Peoples living in North America prior to European contact.
u1228 (x=58) 5 – U1.1.1
u1229 (x=144) Use maps to locate peoples in the Eastern Woodland (the Woodland
u1230 (x=58) Peoples east of the Mississippi River), desert Southwest, the Pacific Northwest, and the nomadic nations of the Great Plains.
u1231 (x=58) 5 – U1.1.2
u1232 (x=144) Compare how Indigenous Peoples in the Eastern Woodland and
u1233 (x=58) another tribal region adapted to or modified the environment.
u1234 (x=58) 5 – U1.1.3
u1235 (x=144) Describe Eastern Woodland life with respect to governmental and
u1236 (x=58) family structures, trade, and their relationship to the land.
u1237 U1.2 European Exploration
u1238 Identify the causes and consequences of European exploration and colonization.
u1239 (x=59) 5 – U1.2.1
u1240 (x=144) Explain the technological and political developments that made sea
u1241 (x=59) exploration possible.
u1242 Examples may include but are not limited to: the invention of the astrolabe, improved maps, the rise of nation-states.
u1243 (x=58) 5 – U1.2.2
u1244 (x=144) Use case studies of individual explorers and stories of life in Europe
u1245 (x=58) to compare the goals, obstacles, motivations, and consequences for European exploration and colonization of the Americas.
u1246 Examples may include but are not limited to: the economic, political, cultural, and religious consequences of colonization, including who was impacted.
u1247 U1.3 African Life Before the 16th Century
u1248 Describe the lives of peoples living in West Africa prior to the 16th century.
u1249 (x=58) 5 – U1.3.1
u1250 (x=144) Use maps to locate the major regions of Africa (North Africa, West
u1251 (x=58) Africa, Central Africa, East Africa, Southern Africa).
u1252 (x=58) 5 – U1.3.2
u1253 (x=144) Describe the life and cultural development of people living in West
u1254 (x=58) Africa before the 16th century with respect to economic (the ways people made a living) and family structures, and the growth of states, towns, and trade.

u1258 U1.4 Three World Interactions

u1260 (x=58) 5 – U1.4.1
u1261 (x=144) Describe the convergence of Europeans, Indigenous Peoples, and
u1262 (x=58) Africans in the Americas after 1492 from the perspective of these three groups.

u1273 U2 USHG ERA 2 – COLONIZATION AND SETTLEMENT (1585-1763)
u1274 Individually and collaboratively, students will engage in planned inquiries to understand how European values and institutions transferred to and modified in the colonies, and how slavery reshaped European and African life in the Americas.
u1275 U2.1 European Struggle for Control of North America
u1276 Compare the regional settlement patterns and describe significant developments in Southern, New England, and the Mid-Atlantic colonies.
u1277 (x=58) 5 – U2.1.1
u1278 (x=144) Describe significant developments in the Southern colonies, including:
u1279 • patterns of settlement and control, including the impact of geography (land forms and climate) on settlement.
u1280 • the establishment of Jamestown.
u1281 • the development of one-crop economies (plantation land use and growing season for rice in Carolinas and tobacco in Virginia).
u1282 • interactions with Indigenous Peoples, including the trading of goods, services, and ideas among Europeans and Indigenous Peoples.
u1283 • the development of colonial representative assemblies (House of Burgesses).
u1284 • the development of slavery.
u1285 (x=58) 5 – U2.1.2
u1286 (x=144) Describe significant developments in the New England colonies,
u1287 (x=58) including:
u1288 • patterns of settlement and control including the impact of geography (land forms and climate) on settlement.
=== page 46 === [running header: Michigan K-12 Social Studies Standards | MICHIGAN DEPARTMENT OF EDUCATION]
u1289 (x=14) 46 •
u1290 (x=86) interactions with Indigenous Peoples, including the trading of goods, services, and ideas among Europeans and Indigenous Peoples, growth of agricultural (small farms) and non-agricultural (shipping, manufacturing) economies.
u1291 • the development of government, including the establishment of town meetings, development of colonial legislatures, and growth of royal government.
u1292 • religious tensions in Massachusetts that led to the establishment of other colonies in New England.
u1293 (x=59) 5 – U2.1.3
u1294 (x=144) Describe significant developments in the Middle colonies, including:
u1295 • patterns of settlement and control, including the impact of geography (land forms and climate) on settlement.
u1296 • interactions with Indigenous Peoples, including the trading of goods, services, and ideas among Europeans and Indigenous Peoples.
u1297 • the growth of economies in the Middle colonies, the Dutch settlement in New Netherlands, Quaker settlement in Pennsylvania, and subsequent English takeover of the Middle colonies.
u1298 • immigration patterns leading to ethnic diversity in the Middle colonies.
u1299 (x=59) 5 – U2.1.4
u1300 (x=144) Compare the regional settlement patterns of the Southern colonies,
u1301 (x=59) New England, and the Middle colonies.
u1302 (x=59) 5 – U2.1.5
u1303 (x=144) Explain the economic, political, cultural, and religious causes of
u1304 (x=59) migration to colonial North America.
u1305 U2.2 European Slave Trade and Slavery in Colonial America

u1309 • the trade routes.
u1310 • the people and goods that were traded.
u1311 • the Middle Passage.
u1312 • the impact on life in Africa.
u1313 (x=59) 5 – U2.2.2

u1318 (x=59) of their diverse African histories and cultures to develop distinct African-American identities.

u1340 U3 USHG ERA 3 REVOLUTION AND THE NEW NATION (1754-1800)
u1341 Individually and collaboratively, students will engage in planned inquiries to investigate the causes of the American Revolution, the ideas and interests involved in forging the revolutionary movement, and the reasons for the American victory.
u1342 U3.1 Causes of the American Revolution
u1343 Identify the major political, economic, and ideological reasons for the American Revolution.
u1344 (x=58) 5 – U3.1.1
u1345 (x=144) Describe how the French and Indian War affected British policy toward
u1346 (x=58) the colonies and subsequent colonial dissatisfaction with the new policy.
u1347 (x=58) 5 – U3.1.2
u1348 (x=144) Describe the causes and effects of events such as the Stamp Act, the
u1349 (x=58) Boston Massacre, the Boston Tea Party, and the Intolerable Acts.
u1350 (x=58) 5 – U3.1.3
u1351 (x=144) Using an event from the Revolutionary era, explain how British and
u1352 (x=58) colonial views on authority and the use of power without authority differed (views on representative government).
u1353 Examples may include but are not limited to: the Boston Tea Party, quartering of soldiers, writs of assistance, the closing of colonial legislatures.
u1354 (x=58) 5 – U3.1.4
u1355 (x=144) Describe the role of the First and Second Continental Congresses in
u1356 (x=58) unifying the colonies.
u1357 Examples may include but are not limited to: addressing the Intolerable Acts, declaring independence, drafting the Articles of Confederation.
u1358 (x=58) 5 – U3.1.5
u1359 (x=144) Use the Declaration of Independence to explain why many colonists
u1360 (x=58) wanted to separate from Great Britain and why they believed they had the right to do so.
=== page 48 === [running header: Michigan K-12 Social Studies Standards | MICHIGAN DEPARTMENT OF EDUCATION]
u1361 (x=14) 48 5 – U3.1.6
u1362 (x=144) Identify the role that key individuals played in leading the colonists
u1363 (x=58) to revolution, including George Washington, Thomas Jefferson, Benjamin Franklin, Patrick Henry, Samuel Adams, John Adams, and Thomas Paine.
u1364 (x=58) 5 – U3.1.7
u1365 (x=144) Describe how colonial experiences with self-government and ideas
u1366 (x=58) about government influenced the decision to declare independence.
u1367 Examples may include but are not limited to: Mayflower Compact, House of Burgesses and town meetings; the Iroquois Confederacy; protecting individual rights and promoting the common good; natural rights; limited government; representative government.
u1368 (x=58) 5 – U3.1.8
u1369 (x=144) Identify a problem that people in the colonies faced, identify
u1370 (x=58) alternative choices for addressing the problem with possible consequences, and describe the course of action taken.
u1371 U3.2 The American Revolution and its Consequences
u1372 Explain the multi-faceted nature of the American Revolution and its consequences.
u1373 (x=58) 5 – U3.2.1
u1374 (x=144) Describe the advantages and disadvantages each side had during
u1375 (x=58) the American Revolution with respect to military leadership, geography, types of resources, and motivations.
u1376 (x=58) 5 – U3.2.2
u1377 (x=144) Describe the importance of Valley Forge, the Battle of Saratoga, and
u1378 (x=58) the Battle of Yorktown in the American Revolution.
u1379 (x=59) 5 – U3.2.3
u1380 (x=144) Investigate the role of women, enslaved and freed Africans,
u1381 (x=59) Indigenous Peoples, and France in helping shape the outcome of the war.
u1382 (x=59) 5 – U3.2.4
u1383 (x=144) Describe the significance of the Treaty of Paris (establishment of the
u1384 (x=59) United States and its initial boundaries).
u1385 U3.3 Creating New Government(s) and a New Constitution
u1386 Explain some of the challenges faced by the new nation under the Articles of Confederation, and analyze the development of the Constitution as a new plan for governing.
u1387 (x=59) 5 – U3.3.1
u1388 (x=144) Describe the powers of the national government and state
u1389 (x=59) governments under the Articles of Confederation.
u1390 (x=59) 5 – U3.3.2
u1391 (x=144) Give examples of problems the country faced under the Articles of
u1392 (x=59) Confederation.
u1393 Examples may include but are not limited to: lack of national army, competing currencies, reliance on state governments for money.
u1394 (x=59) 5 – U3.3.3
u1395 (x=144) Explain why the Constitutional Convention was convened and why the

u1457 THE ARC OF INQUIRY: GRADES 6-8
u1458 Dimension 1: Developing Questions and Planning Inquiries Central to a rich social studies experience is the capability for developing questions that can frame and advance an inquiry. Those questions come in two forms: compelling and supporting questions.
u1459 Individually and collaboratively, students construct compelling questions and:
u1460 • explain why compelling questions are important to others (e.g., peers, adults).
u1461 • identify disciplinary concepts and ideas associated with a compelling question that are open to different interpretations.
u1462 • identify the disciplinary concepts and ideas associated with a supporting question that are open to interpretation.
u1463 • explain how supporting questions help answer compelling questions in an inquiry.
u1464 • determine the kinds of sources that will be helpful in answering compelling and supporting questions, taking into consideration the different opinions people have about how to answer the questions.
u1465 Dimension 2: Applying Disciplinary Concepts and Tools The four disciplines within social studies provide the intellectual context for studying how humans have interacted with each other and with the environment over time. Each of these disciplines — civics, economics, geography, and history — offers a unique way of thinking and organizing knowledge as well as systems for verifying knowledge. Dimension 2 focuses on the disciplinary concepts and tools students need to understand and apply as they study the specific content described in Michigan’s state standards.
u1466 Dimension 3: Evaluating Sources and Using Evidence Dimension 3 includes the skills students need to analyze information and come to conclusions in an inquiry. These skills focus on gathering and evaluating sources, and then developing claims and using evidence to support these claims.
u1467 Individually and collaboratively, students:
u1468 • gather relevant information from multiple sources while using the origin, structure, and context to guide the selection.
u1469 • use distinctions among fact and opinion to determine the credibility of multiple sources.
u1470 • identify evidence that draws information from multiple sources in response to compelling questions.
u1471 • use evidence to develop claims in response to compelling questions.
u1472 Dimension 4: Communicating Conclusions and Taking Informed Action Students should construct and communicate claims for a variety of purposes and audiences. These audiences may range from the school classroom to the larger public community.
u1473 Individually and collaboratively, students:
u1474 • construct arguments using claims and evidence from multiple sources.
u1475 • construct explanations using reasoning, correct sequence, examples, and details with relevant information and data.
u1476 • present a summary of arguments and explanations to others outside the classroom using print and oral technologies (posters, essays, letters, debates, speeches, and reports) and digital technologies (Internet, social media, digital documentary).
u1477 • critique arguments.
u1478 • critique explanations.
u1479 • draw on disciplinary concepts to explain the challenges people have faced and opportunities they have created, in addressing local, regional, and global problems at various times and places.
u1480 • explain different strategies and approaches that students and others could take in working alone and together to address local, regional, and global problems, and predict possible results of their actions.
u1481 • use a range of deliberative and democratic procedures to make decisions about and act on civic problems in their classrooms and schools.
=== page 52 === [running header: Michigan K-12 Social Studies Standards | MICHIGAN DEPARTMENT OF EDUCATION]
u1483 SOCIAL STUDIES PROCESS AND SKILLS STANDARDS: GRADES 6-8
u1484 P1 READING AND COMMUNICATION – READ AND COMMUNICATE EFFECTIVELY
u1485 P1.1 Use appropriate strategies to read and interpret basic social science tables, graphs, graphics, maps, and texts.
u1486 P1.2 Interpret primary and secondary source documents for point of view, context, bias, and frame of ref erence or perspective.
u1487 P1.3 Express social science ideas clearly in written, spoken, and graphic forms, including tables, line graphs, bar graphs, pie charts, maps, and images.
u1488 P1.4 Present an argument supported with evidence.
u1489 P2 INQUIRY, RESEARCH, AND ANALYSIS
u1490 P2.1 Use compelling and supporting questions to investigate social scientific problems.
u1491 P2.2 Evaluate data presented in social science tables, graphs, graphics, maps, and texts.
u1492 P2.3 Know how to find, organize, and interpret information from a variety of sources.
u1493 P2.4 Use resources in multiple forms and from multiple perspectives to analyze issues.
u1494 P3 PUBLIC DISCOURSE AND DECISION MAKING
u1495 P3.1 Clearly state an issue as a question of public policy, gather and interpret information about that is sue, and generate and evaluate possible alternative resolutions.
u1496 P3.2 Discuss public policy issues, clarifying position, considering opposing views, and applying Democratic Values or Constitutional Principles to develop and refine claims.
u1497 P3.3 Construct arguments expressing and justifying decisions on public policy issues supported with evi dence.
u1498 P3.4 Explain the challenges people have faced and actions they have taken to address issues at different times and places.
u1499 P4 CIVIC PARTICIPATION
u1500 P4.1 Act out of the rule of law and hold others to the same standard.
u1501 P4.2 Assess options for individuals and groups to plan and conduct activities intended to advance views on matters of public policy.
u1502 P4.3 Plan, conduct, and evaluate the effectiveness of activities intended to advance views on matters of public policy.

u1506 WORLD GEOGRAPHY: GRADE 6
u1507 Sixth-grade students will explore the tools and mental constructs used by geographers as they study contemporary world geography. Contemporary civics/government and economics content is integrated throughout the year. As a capstone, the students will conduct an investigation of a global issue. Using knowledge, research, and inquiry, they will analyze an issue and propose a plan for the future, including a persuasive essay.

u1541 WORLD GEOGRAPHY: GRADE SIX
u1542 GEOGRAPHY
u1543 G1 THE WORLD IN SPATIAL TERMS: GEOGRAPHICAL HABITS OF MIND
u1544 The use of technology has dramatically enhanced the ability of teachers and students to see the world in different ways. Geo-spatial technology includes geographic information systems (GIS), remote sensing, and global positioning systems (GPS), and the ability to look at places all around the world has allowed students to do geography, not just learn it.
u1545 Learning how to use technology is only part of learning to think spatially. Geographically literate people: know about our complex interconnected world; understand science and social science concepts; use maps, data and geo-spatial technologies; and use spatial reasoning. Spatial reasoning involves the following: looking at patterns; analyzing con nections between places; understanding how the conditions at one place can be similar or very different from another; trying to understand how location is important; and see ing why some characteristics tend to occur together in places.
u1546 Geographers also look at the world with an ecological perspective. What are the relation ships within ecosystems, and what role do humans have in using, modifying, and adapt ing to different environments from a local to global scale?
u1547 G1.1 Spatial Thinking
u1548 Use maps and other geographic tools to acquire and process information from a spatial perspective.
u1549 (x=58) 6 – G1.1.1
u1550 (x=144) Use a variety of geographic tools (maps, globes, and web-based geog
u1551 (x=58) raphy technology) to analyze the world at global, regional, and local scales.
u1552 Examples may include but are not limited to: looking for the significance of location, making comparisons among places and regions, identifying spatial patterns and comparing patterns, exploring how places and people are connected as well as how people are part of, use, and impact the environment. Spatial analysis can also involve looking at an issue at different scales in order to provide different insights.
u1553 (x=58) 6 – G1.1.2
u1554 (x=144) Draw a sketch map, or add information to an outline map, of the world
u1555 (x=58) or a world region.
u1556 Examples may include but are not limited to: locate the following on a world map: the United States, North and South America, Africa, Europe, and Asia; conti nents; oceans; and latitude lines — equator, Prime Meridian, tropics of Cancer and Capricorn, Arctic and Antarctic circles using Geographic Information Systems (GIS), drawing, or web-based programs.

u1584 56 G1.3 Geographical Understanding
u1585 The purpose of middle school geography curriculum is to develop content, themes, skills, and perspectives that can help students understand a diverse and interconnected world.
u1586 (x=58) 6 – G1.3.1
u1587 (x=144) Use the fundamental themes of geography (location, place, human-en
u1588 (x=58) vironment interaction, movement, region) to describe regions or places on earth.
u1589 Examples may include but are not limited to:
u1590 • “place” deals with the natural and human characteristics of a place while “loca tion” deals with where the place is, especially relative to other places.
u1591 • “human-environment interaction” deals with resources, human adaptation, and human impact, as well as natural catastrophes.
u1592 • “movement” includes migration of people, transportation of goods and services, and the diffusion of information, as well as the movement of material in natural cycles, such as water through hydrology.
u1593 • “regions” are generalizations about the common characteristics of areas.
u1594 (x=58) 6 – G1.3.2
u1595 (x=144) Explain the different ways in which places are connected and how
u1596 (x=58) those connections demonstrate interdependence and accessibility.
u1597 G2 PLACES AND REGIONS
u1598 Describe the cultural groups and diversities among people who are rooted in particular places and in human constructs called regions. Analyze the physical and human charac teristics of places and regions.
u1599 G2.1 Physical Characteristics of Places
u1600 Describe the physical characteristics of places.
u1601 (x=58) 6 – G2.1.1
u1602 (x=144) Locate and describe the basic patterns of landforms.
u1603 Examples may include but are not limited to: patterns at a continental scale or larger.
u1604 (x=58) 6 – G2.1.2
u1605 (x=144) Locate and describe the basic patterns and processes of plate tectonics.
u1606 Examples may include but are not limited to: the location of continental plates and the Ring of Fire. Processes include plate movement, uplift, earthquakes, and vol canism.
u1607 (x=58) 6 – G2.1.3
u1608 (x=144) Locate and describe the characteristics and patterns of major world
u1609 (x=58) climates and ecosystems.
u1610 Examples may include but are not limited to: tropical wet and tropical wet-dry, arid and semi-arid, sub-tropical, continental, and arctic climates. Ecosystems include tropical rain forest, savanna, grassland, desert, temperate and coniferous forests, tundra, oceans, and ice caps.

Additional examples might include how groups of people with different levels of eco nomic or political power might choose or be restricted to different locations.

u1650 G4 HUMAN SYSTEMS
u1651 Explain that human activities may be seen on Earth’s surface. Human systems include the way people divide the land, decide where to live, develop communities that are part of the larger cultural mosaic, and engage in the cultural diffusion of ideas and products within and among groups.
u1652 G4.1 Cultural Mosaic
u1653 Describe the characteristics, distribution, and complexity of Earth’s cultural mosaic.
u1654 (x=58) 6 – G4.1.1
u1655 (x=144) Define culture and describe examples of cultural change through dif
u1656 (x=58) fusion, including what has diffused, why and where it has spread, and positive and negative consequences of the change.
u1657 Examples may include but are not limited to: describing the spread of busi nesses such as fast food franchises, sports like karate or soccer, products like athletic shoes, languages like English, or diseases like the Zika virus.
u1658 (x=58) 6 – G4.1.2
u1659 (x=144) Compare and contrast the gender roles assigned to men and women in
u1660 (x=58) different societies.
u1661 Examples may include but are not limited to: how different religions and/or na tions assign, expect, or require different roles for men and women, such as who can vote, own property, or hold office. Note that gender roles are culturally defined and vary widely. Within a culture, the majority of traditional roles have varying degrees of acceptance and change over time.
u1662 (x=58) 6 – G4.1.3
u1663 (x=144) Describe cultures of the region being studied, including the major lan
u1664 (x=58) guages and religions.
u1665 (x=58) 6 – G4.1.4
u1666 (x=144) Explain how culture influences the daily lives of people.
u1667 Examples may include but are not limited to: how people make a living, raise families, educate children and practice their religion in different cultures and commu nities.
u1668 G4.2 Technology Patterns and Networks
u1669 Describe how technology creates patterns and networks that connect people, resourc es, products, and ideas.

u1674 (x=144) Identify and describe the advantages, disadvantages, and impacts
u1675 (x=58) of different technologies used to transport people and products, and spread ideas throughout the world.
u1676 Examples may include but are not limited to: the advantages and disadvan tages of trucks, trains, ships, and planes for transporting people and/or material; the advantages and disadvantages of print, radio, television, the Internet, and social media for moving information.
u1677 G4.3 Patterns of Human Settlement
u1678 Describe patterns, processes, and functions of human settlement.
u1679 (x=58) 6 – G4.3.1

A technology example might be how irrigation tech nology changed farming in the Great Plains or how the Green Revolution changed farming in Asia.
u1683 (x=58) 6 – G4.3.2
u1684 (x=144) Describe patterns of settlement and explain why people settle where
u1685 (x=58) they do and how people make their livings.

u1687 (x=58) 6 – G4.3.3
u1688 (x=144) Explain the patterns, causes, and consequences of major human
u1689 (x=58) migrations.
u1690 Examples may include but are not limited to: refugee migrations, economic mi grations, seasonal migration, and migrations from rural to urban.
u1691 G4.4 Forces of Cooperation and Conflict
u1692 Explain how forces of cooperation and conflict among people influence the division of the Earth’s surface and its resources.
u1693 (x=58) 6 – G4.4.1
u1694 (x=144) Identify factors that contribute to cooperation and conflict between and
u1695 (x=58) among cultural groups (control/use of natural resources, power, wealth, and cultural diversity).
u1696 (x=58) 6 – G4.4.2
u1697 (x=144) Evaluate examples of cooperation and conflict within the region under
u1698 (x=58) study from different perspectives.
u1699 Examples may include but are not limited to: cooperation between the United States and Canada to protect the fresh water of the Great Lakes, cooperation efforts to stop the spread of diseases among populations, or conflict over control of islands in the South China Sea.

u1700 60 G5 ENVIRONMENT AND SOCIETY
u1701 Explain that the physical environment is modified by human activities, which are influ enced by the ways in which human societies value and use the Earth’s natural resources, and by Earth’s physical features and processes. Explain how human action modifies the physical environment and how physical systems affect human systems.
u1702 G5.1 Humans and the Environment
u1703 Describe how humans use and modify the environment.
u1704 (x=58) 6 – G5.1.1
u1705 (x=144) Describe examples of how humans have impacted and are continuing
u1706 (x=58) to impact the environment in different places as a consequence of population size, resource use, level of consumption, and technology.
u1707 Examples may include but are not limited to: how population pressure impacts deforestation in Brazil, how higher standards of living increase pollution in China, how the use of plastics in the United States can impact water resources, and how use of fossil fuels leads to climate change.
u1708 (x=58) 6 – G5.1.2
u1709 (x=144) Explain how different technologies can have positive and negative im
u1710 (x=58) pacts on the environment.
u1711 Examples may include but are not limited to: water management, energy ex amples include advantages and disadvantages of wind and solar power generation, as well as fracking and tar sands mining; transportation examples might include road and rail transportation and expansion of cities; agricultural examples might include terracing, deforestation, or the use of pesticides and herbicides.
u1712 (x=58) 6 – G5.1.3
u1713 (x=144) Analyze ways in which human-induced changes in the physical envi
u1714 (x=58) ronment in one place can cause changes in other places.
u1715 Examples may include but are not limited to: how cutting forests in one region may result in flooding downstream, how plastic litter in the watershed leads to lake and ocean pollution, how over-fertilization and phosphate use can lead to changes in water quality, and how different factors lead to global climate change, which may impact regions differently.
u1716 (x=58) 6 – G5.1.4
u1717 (x=144) Define natural resources and explain how people in different places
u1718 (x=58) use, define, and acquire resources in different ways.
u1719 G5.2 Physical and Human Systems
u1720 Describe how physical and human systems shape patterns on the Earth’s surface.
u1721 (x=58) 6 – G5.2.1
u1722 (x=144) Analyze the effects that a change in the physical environment could
u1723 (x=58) have on human activities and the actions people would be required to make (or would choose to make) in response to the change.
u1724 Examples may include but are not limited to: how drought in Africa and Syria is leading to emigration, how coral bleaching is leading to reduced tourism in Australia and the Caribbean, how earthquakes are leading to revised building codes, or how sea level rise is leading to coastal flooding and barrier construction.
=== page 61 ===
u1728 (x=58) 6 – G5.2.2
u1729 (x=144) Analyze how combinations of human decisions and natural forces can
u1730 (x=58) lead to (or help people avoid) a natural disaster.
u1731 Examples may include but are not limited to: how building in flood plains in creases the likelihood of a natural disaster, and how the federal Soil Conservation Service works to prevent a natural disaster, such as the Dust Bowl.
u1732 G6 GLOBAL ISSUES
u1733 A global issue is one that has an impact affecting many regions of the world.
u1734 G6.1 Global Topic Investigation and Issue Analysis
u1735 (x=58) 6 – G6.1.1
u1736 (x=144) Identify global issues.
u1737 Examples may include but are not limited to: natural disasters, immigration, food production, food distribution, the impact of climate change, population growth, resource use and depletion, meeting the needs of refugees, migration, poverty, eco nomic development, conflict, and terrorism.
u1738 (x=58) 6 – G6.1.2
u1739 (x=144) Investigate a contemporary global issue by applying the skills of geo
u1740 (x=58) graphic inquiry.
u1741 Examples may include but are not limited to: asking geographic questions; acquiring, organizing, and analyzing geographic information; answering geographic questions when practical; using inquiry methods to acquire content knowledge and appropriate data about the issue; identifying the causes and consequences and ana lyzing the impact, both positive and negative.
u1742 (x=58) 6 – G6.1.3
u1743 (x=144) Develop a plan for action:
u1744 • share and discuss findings of research and issue analysis in group discussions and debates.
u1745 • compose a persuasive essay justifying a position with a reasoned argument.
u1746 • develop an action plan to address or inform others about the issue, at local to global scales.

u1747 62 CIVICS AND GOVERNMENT
u1748 C1 PURPOSES OF GOVERNMENT
u1749 Analyze how people identify, organize, and accomplish the purposes of government.
u1750 C1.1 Nature of Civic Life, Politics, and Government
u1751 Describe civic life, politics, and government and explain their relationships.
u1752 (x=58) 6 – C1.1.1
u1753 (x=144) Compare and contrast different ideas about the purposes of govern
u1754 (x=58) ment in different nations, nation-states or governments.
u1755 Examples may include but are not limited to: protecting individual rights, promoting the common good, providing economic security, molding the character of citizens, or promoting a particular religion. Purposes may also include keeping an ethnic group or party in power. Governments may include those of nation-states, newly independent states, emerging states, and other governmental entities such as tribal governments.
u1756 C3 STRUCTURE AND FUNCTIONS OF GOVERNMENT
u1757 Explain that governments are structured to serve the people. Describe the major activi ties of government, including making and enforcing laws, providing services and benefits to individuals and groups, assigning individual and collective responsibilities, generating revenue, and providing national security.
u1758 C3.6 Characteristics of Nation-States
u1759 Describe the characteristics of nation-states and how they may interact.
u1760 (x=58) 6 – C3.6.1
u1761 (x=144) Define the characteristics of modern nation-states.
u1762 Examples may include but are not limited to: a specific territory, clearly defined boundaries, citizens, collect taxes and provide services, jurisdiction over people who reside there, laws, and government.
u1763 (x=58) 6 – C3.6.2
u1764 (x=144) Compare and contrast various forms of government around the world.
u1765 Examples may include but are not limited to: democracies, parliamentary sys tems, dictatorships, oligarchies, and theocracies.
u1766 C4 RELATIONSHIP OF UNITED STATES TO OTHER GOVERNMENTS, WORLD ISSUES, AND WORLD GOVERNING ORGANIZATIONS
u1767 Explain ways in which governments interact with one another through trade, diplomacy, treaties and agreements, humanitarian aid, economic sanctions and incentives, military force, and the threat of force.
u1768 C4.3 Conflict and Cooperation Between and Among Nations
u1769 Explain the various ways that governments interact both positively and negatively.
u1770 (x=58) 6 – C4.3.1
u1771 (x=144) Explain how governments address national and international issues
u1772 (x=58) and form policies, and how the policies may not be consistent with those of other nation-states.
=== page 63 ===
u1776 (x=58) Examples may include but are not limited to: climate change, and human and civil rights; within the United States, federal/tribal relations in the United States.
u1777 (x=58) 6 – C4.3.2
u1778 (x=144) Explain the challenges to governments to address global issues, and
u1779 (x=58) the international cooperation needed to do so.
u1780 (x=58) 6 – C4.3.3
u1781 (x=144) Analyze the impact of treaties, agreements, and international organi
u1782 (x=58) zations on global issues.
u1783 Examples may include but are not limited to: the North American Free Trade Agreement (NAFTA) or subsequent agreements, the North Atlantic Treaty Organiza tion (NATO), the Organization of American States (OAS), the United Nations (UN), the Universal Declaration of Human Rights, and the Paris Climate Accord.
u1784 ECONOMICS

u1791 (x=58) change the decision-making process.
u1792 Examples may include but are not limited to: acquiring money, profit, and goods; wanting to avoid loss of position in society; job placement; taxes on ciga rettes to discourage smoking; raising prices to increase profit.

u1798 (x=144) Analyze the impact of sanctions, tariffs, treaties, quotas, and
u1799 (x=58) subsidies.
u1800 Examples may include but are not limited to: implications of economic sanc tions on all countries involved.

u1849 WORLD HISTORY AND GEOGRAPHY: GRADE 7
u1850 Seventh-grade students will review the tools and mental constructs used by historians and geographers. They will develop an understanding of World History, Eras 1–4. Geography, Civics/Government, and Economics content is integrated throughout the year. As a capstone, the students will conduct investigations about past and present global issues. Using significant content knowledge, research, and inquiry, they will analyze the issue and propose a plan for the future. As part of the inquiry, they will compose civic, persuasive essays using reasoned argument.

u1950 WHG ERA 2 – EARLY CIVILIZATIONS AND CULTURES AND THE EMERGENCE OF PASTORAL PEOPLES, 4000 TO 1000 BCE AND WESTERN HEMISPHERE 4000 BCE to 1500 CE
u1951 Describe and compare defining characteristics of early civilization and pastoral societies, where they emerged, and how they spread. In addition, examples may include forms of non-verbal communication from pictographs to abstract writing (governmental ad ministration, laws, codes, history, and artistic expressions).

Cultural diffusion in volves identifying the innovation, how it is being spread, who the adopters are, and the intended or unintended consequences of the innovation.

u1977 W3 WHG ERA 3 – CLASSICAL TRADITIONS, WORLD RELIGIONS, AND MAJOR EMPIRES, 1000 BCE TO 300 CE
u1978 Analyze classical civilizations and empires and the emergence of major world religions and large-scale empires. During this era, innovations and social, political, and economic changes occurred through the emergence of classical civilizations in Africa, Eurasia, and the Americas. Africa and Eurasia moved in the direction of human interchange as a re sult of trade, empire building, and the diffusion of skills and ideas. Similar interactions occurred in the Americas. Six of the world’s major faiths and ethical systems emerged and classical civilizations established institutions, systems of thought, and cultural styles that would influence neighboring peoples and endure for centuries.

u2018 W3.2 Growth and Development of World Religions
u2019 Explain how world religions or belief systems of Hinduism, Judaism, Buddhism, Christi-anity, Confucianism, Sikhism, and Islam grew and their significance (Sikhism and Islam are included here even though they came after 300 CE). The world’s major faiths and ethical systems emerged, establishing institutions, systems of thought, and cultural styles that would influence neighboring peoples and endure for centuries.
u2020 (x=58) 7 – W3.2.1
u2021 (x=144) Identify and describe the core beliefs of major world religions and be-
u2022 (x=58) lief systems, including Hinduism, Judaism, Buddhism, Christianity, Confucianism, Sikhism and Islam.
u2023 Examples may include, but are not limited to: comparing major figures, sa- cred texts, and basic beliefs (ethnic vs. universalizing; monotheistic vs. polytheistic) among religions; case studies of continuity of local indigenous belief systems or an- imistic religions; comparisons with religious traditions that developed after 1500 CE such as Protestantism.
u2024 (x=58) 7 – W3.2.2
u2025 (x=144) Locate the geographical center of major religions and map the spread
u2026 (x=58) through 1500 CE.

u2062 (x=36) G3 INVESTIGATION AND ANALYSIS
u2063 Throughout the school year, the students are introduced to topics that address issues that integrate time and place. Included are capstone projects that entail the investiga tion of historical issues that have significance for the student and are clearly linked to the world outside the classroom. The topics and issues are developed as possible cap stone projects within units and at the end of the course.

u2069 Era 2 Examples may include but are not limited to: agriculture (investigate the development of different forms of early or contemporary agriculture and its role in helping societies produce enough food for people, and the consequences of agricul ture.
u2070 Era 3 Examples may include but are not limited to: trade (investigate the im pact of trade and trade routes on civilizations) or power (analyze common factors that influence the rise and fall of empires).

Plan and conduct activities intended to advance views on matters of public policy, report the results, and evalu ate effectiveness:
u2122 • identify public policy issues related to global topics and issues studied.
